<div class="calibre6">

 

</div>

<span class="calibre7 bold bold">7. THE WORLD IS SPIKY</span>

<div class="calibre14">

 

</div>

> <div class="calibre11">
>
> <span class="calibre4">The World is Spiky: I stole this delightful
> title from Richard Florida’s article with Tim Gulden in
> <span class="italic">The Atlantic,</span> October 2005.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">“Our dollar looks the same”: Daniel Gross, “The
> Value of a New York Dollar,” <span class="italic">New York,</span>
> November 6, 2006.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">The bottom line: Gross, “The Value of a New
> York Dollar.”</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Ed Glaeser, the Harvard-based economist: Edward
> Glaeser, “Are Cities Dying?” <span class="italic">Journal of Economic
> Perspectives</span> 12, no. 2(spring 1998): 139–60.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">“Who needs a network?”: Jeff Jarvis, “Points to
> Forbes,” blog posting,
> www.buzzmachine.com/2007/04/23/points-to-forbes.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">“Great are the advantages”: Alfred Marshall,
> <span class="italic">Principles of Economics,</span> eighth ed.
> (London: Macmillan, 1920), book 4, chapter 10, www.econ
> lib.org/LIBRARY/Marshall/marP24.html.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Marshall’s idea was intuitive: Marshall’s idea
> was also mathematically convenient. David Warsh,
> <span class="italic">Knowledge and the Wealth of Nations</span> (New
> York: Norton, 2006), chapter 7, explains the mathematical appeal of
> Marshall’s “externalities.” Economists were starting to realize that
> contrary to the dismal predictions of Thomas Malthus, the world was
> getting richer rather than running out of everything. The explanation
> was “increasing returns.” The world wasn’t running out of food or
> energy or space. Instead, more people, more investment, and larger
> firms made things cheaper. Marshall realized that if individual firms
> enjoyed increasing returns to scale, the mathematics of modeling them
> would be formidably complicated, and logically the world would be
> dominated by monopolists. So he made increasing returns a matter of
> technological change “in the air”—externalities—greatly simplifying
> the mathematics and preserving the basic idea of competition. The idea
> was the only way economists could really think about technological
> change until Avinash Dixit, Joseph Stiglitz, and especially Paul Romer
> worked out how to model increasing returns and then technological
> change inside competitive firms.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">The idea of using rents: Warsh,
> <span class="italic">Knowledge and the Wealth of Nations,</span>
> chapter 18, gives a great account of the Lucas lecture and its
> importance. The lecture was eventually published as “On the Mechanics
> of Economic Development,” <span class="italic">Journal of Monetary
> Economics</span> 22 (1988): 3–42.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">When, in 1959: Interview with Gary Becker,
> September 2005. See “It’s the Humanity, Stupid,”
> <span class="italic">FT Magazine</span>.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Ed Glaeser found it: Edward Glaeser and David
> Maré, “Cities and Skills,” NBER Working Paper 4728, May 1994.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Studying the official records: It may be an
> underestimate because Jaffe and his colleagues eliminated cases where,
> say, an IBM patent cited an earlier IBM patent, because they weren’t
> sure whether this sort of citation was just cheap talk or suggested
> some real inspiration from the earlier patent. But later work by
> Jaffe, Trajtenberg, and Bronwyn Hall suggested strongly that
> self-citations indicated real innovative value and weren’t cheap talk
> at all. The original paper is Adam B. Jaffe, Manuel Trajtenberg, and
> Rebecca Henderson, “Geographic Localization of Knowledge Spillovers as
> Evidenced by Patent Citations,” <span class="italic">Quarterly Journal
> of Economics</span> 108, no. 3(August 1993): 577–98. I also
> interviewed Adam Jaffe in November 2006.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">To see the reason: The canonical model of this
> argument is the paper that launched the so-called New Economic
> Geography, Paul Krugman’s elegant “Increasing Returns and Economic
> Geography,” <span class="italic">Journal of Political Economy</span>
> 99, no. 3(June 1991): 483–99. This and many other Krugman academic
> papers are available at math.stanford.edu/~lekheng/
> krugman/index.html.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Looking at the location: David B. Audretsch and
> Maryann P. Feldman, “R&D Spillovers and the Geography of Innovation
> and Production,” <span class="italic">American Economic Review</span>
> 86, no. 3(June 1996): 630–40.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">There are those that: Robert Wright, “China
> Leads on Sorting of Goods Prior to Shipping,”
> <span class="italic">Financial Times,</span> March 27, 2007.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Apart from these easily transportable: Jane
> Jacobs discusses these points at length in two short books,
> <span class="italic">Cities and the Wealth of Nations</span> (New
> York: Random House, 1984), and <span class="italic">The Economy of
> Cities</span> (New York: Random House, 1969). Paul Krugman provides an
> updated discussion in the last chapter of <span class="italic">Pop
> Internationalism</span> (Cambridge: MIT Press, 1996).</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">What if these communications technologies: Jess
> Gaspar and Edward Glaeser, “Information Technology and the Future of
> Cities,” <span class="italic">Journal of Urban Economics</span> 43,
> no. 1(January 1998): 136–56.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Both in the United States and Japan: Gaspar and
> Glaeser, “Information Technology and the Future of Cities.”</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">As for e-mail: Neil Gandal, Charles King III,
> and Marshall W. Van Alstyne, “Information Technology Use and
> Productivity at the Individual Level,” CEPR Discussion Paper 6260,
> April 2007, www.cepr.org/pubs/ dps/DP6260.asp.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">On niche dating sites: Alex Mindlin, “On Niche
> Dating Sites, Many More Women,” <span class="italic">The New York
> Times,</span> February 26, 2007.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Just look at jointly written academic papers:
> Gaspar and Glaeser, “Information Technology and the Future of
> Cities.”</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Since the 1980s: Gaspar and Glaeser,
> “Information Technology and the Future of Cities.”</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">“She was dissatisfied”: Jane Jacobs,
> <span class="italic">The Economy of Cities,</span> p. 51.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">The business guru Michael Porter: Michael
> Porter, “Clusters and the New Economics of Competition,”
> <span class="italic">Harvard Business Review</span> 76, no. 6
> (November–December 1998): 77–90.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>

>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">A group of four economists: Edward L. Glaeser,
> Hedi D. Kallal, Jose A. Scheinkman, and Andrei Shleifer, “Growth in

> no. 6(December 1992): 1126–52.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Nor is this the only: Gianmarco Ottaviano and
> Giovanni Peri, “The Economic Value of Cultural Diversity: Evidence
> from US Cities,” NBER Working Paper 10904, November 2004, available
> at: ideas.repec.org/a/oup/jecgeo/v6y2006i1p9-44.html.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">When Katrina hit: Jack Shafer, “Don’t Refloat:
> The Case Against Rebuilding the Sunken City of New Orleans,”
> <span class="italic">Slate,</span> September 7, 2005,
> www.slate.com/id/2125810.</span>
>

>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">New Orleans is not the only: Ed Glaeser and
> Janet Kohlhase, “Cities, Regions and the Decline of Tranport Costs,”

>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">In the United Kingdom, Liverpool lost: Alan
> Beattie, “Engine of Enterprise in the Push and Pull of Rural

>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Next to faded glories: A poignant portrait of
> Detroit is Daniel Pimlott, “A Morbid Urban Safari,”
> <span class="italic">Financial Times,</span> July 15, 2006.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">(Perhaps because of a belated outbreak): The
> paper is now called “Urban Decline and Durable Housing.” The story
> about the provocative subtitle is from a profile of Glaeser: Jon
> Gertner, “Homo Economicus,” <span class="italic">The New York Times
> Magazine,</span> March 5, 2006.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">“There’s no builder”: Gertner, “Homo
> Economicus.”</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">The housing stock in pre-Katrina: My source for

> Refloat.”</span>

>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Glaeser was aghast: See Edward Glaeser, “Should

> <span class="italic">Economists’ Voice</span> 2, no. 4(2005), article
> 4.</span>
>

>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Steven Landsburg, writing in
> <span class="italic">Slate:</span> Steven E. Landsburg, “Hurricane
> Relief? Or a \$200,000 Check?” <span class="italic">Slate,</span>
> September 22, 2005, www.slate.com/id/2126715/.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">“Don’t make them go back”: Telephone interview
> with George Horwich, February 2006. In the end, some compensation was
> payable to residents who moved away from their original homes. It
> tended to be less generous; for instance, a federal program would only
> pay 60 percent of the value of a damaged home if the homeowner decided
> to leave Louisiana, but the full value of any damages, up to
> \$150,000, for those who stayed to rebuild. See, for instance, Brett
> Martel, “\$4. 2 Billion for Hurricane Rebuilding Approved,”
> <span class="italic">Houston Chronicle,</span> July 11, 2006,
> www.chron.com/disp/story.mpl/hurricane/4039578.html.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Gyourko reckons: Interview with Joe Gyourko,
> December 2006.</span>

>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">“a utopian environmentalist community”: David
> Owen, “Green Manhattan,” <span class="italic">The New Yorker,</span>
> October 18, 2004.</span>

>
>  
>
> </div>

>

> </div>

</div>

<span class="calibre7 bold bold">8. RATIONAL REVOLUTIONS</span>

<div class="calibre14">

 

</div>

> <div class="calibre11">
>
> <span class="calibre4">The vice president: Gore’s retracted concession
> is detailed on news archives at CNN,
> archives.cnn.com/2000/ALLPOLITICS/stories/12/13/got.here/index.html,
> and PBS,
> www.pbs.org/newshour/media/election2000/election_night.html.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">(For more detail): For the math behind the odds
> of 1 in 300,000 see Jordan Ellenberg, “Why Your Ballot Isn’t
> Meaningless,” <span class="italic">Slate,</span>
> www.slate.com/id/2108029/, and accompanying equations at
> slate.com/Features/pdf/BayesianVote.pdf. Despite the title,
> Ellenberg’s calculations show that your ballot
> <span class="italic">is</span> meaningless. Also see Steven Landsburg,
> “Don’t Vote: Play the Lottery Instead,”
> <span class="italic">Slate,</span> September 29, 2004,
> www.slate.com/id/2107240/. Landsburg’s alternative assumptions make it
> more likely that you would affect a fifty-fifty election and less
> likely you’d affect a less even race. The reason for the difference is
> that Landsburg assumes you know that a fifty-fifty election really is
> that close, while Ellenberg models the process of trying to judge how
> close the election really is. The disagreement is interesting but
> doesn’t alter the conclusion: If you are thinking of affecting a
> presidential race, forget about it.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Contrast the voting decision: Bryan Caplan has
> the clearest explanation of this reasoning—for example, in his essay
> for the Cato Institute, “The Myth of the Rational Voter,” November 6,
> 2006,
> www.cato-unbound.org/2006/11/06/bryan-caplan/the-myth-of-the-rational-voter/,
> or in his book of the same title (Princeton, N.J.: Princeton
> University Press, 2007). Caplan goes further than I do, claiming not
> only that voters are rationally ignorant but that they are “rationally
> irrational”—they actually enjoy holding infantile views about politics
> and know those views cost them nothing. Caplan’s thesis is worth
> taking seriously; he might argue that voters should be bracketed with
> the clients of Mexican prostitutes from chapter 1: They are being
> silly but have no compelling reason to be sensible. In some instances,
> Caplan’s thesis produces different implications from mine. However,
> I’d argue that “rationally irrational” voters and “rationally
> ignorant” voters are unlikely to behave differently in the examples I
> explore in this chapter.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">As an example of that ignorance: My source for
> data on voter ignorance is Kaiser Family Foundation,
> www.kff.org/kaiserpolls/1001-welftbl.cfm, table 16. The size of
> foreign aid spending is from Samuel Bazzi, Sheila Herrling, and
> Stewart Patrick, “Billions for War, Pennies for the Poor,” Center for
> Global Development, March 16, 2007, www.cgdev.org/
> content/publications/detail/13232.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">This government program: John C. Beghin,
> Barbara El Osta, Jay R. Cherlow, and Samarendu Mohanty, “The Cost of
> the U.S. Sugar Program Revisited,” <span class="italic">Contemporary
> Economic Policy</span> 21, no. 1(January 2003): 106–16,
> cep.oxfordjournals.org/cgi/content/abstract/21/1/106.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>

>
> <span class="calibre4">Politically, this is perfect: For more on this,

> politics. The argument originates with Gary Becker, “A Theory of

> <span class="italic">Quarterly Journal of Economics</span> 98, no.

<span class="calibre7 bold bold">9. A MILLION YEARS OF LOGIC</span>

<div class="calibre14">

 

</div>

> <div class="calibre11">
>
> <span class="calibre4">Imagine compressing: For the chronology, I have
> relied on Eric Beinhocker’s summary in <span class="italic">The
> Origins of Wealth</span> (London: Random House, 2007) and
> encyclopedias.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">But economic growth didn’t simply: For the
> Stone Age, I am using population data from Kremer and assuming no
> appreciable increase in income per capita. Michael Kremer, “Population
> Growth and Technological Change: One Million B.C. to 1990,”
> <span class="italic">Quarterly Journal of Economics</span> 108, no.
> 3(1993): 681–716. For modern population growth, see U.S. Census
> Bureau, www.census.gov/ipc/www/idb. Data for after
> <span class="calibre13">A.D.</span> 1 are from Angus Maddison, the
> world’s foremost calculator of historical economic data, at
> www.ggdc.net/maddison/Historical_Statistics/
> horizontal-file_10-2006.xls.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">For example, if you flip: For this data and the
> example, I am relying on an old essay by J. Bradford DeLong for
> chapter two of his forthcoming book, <span class="italic">Slouching
> Towards Utopia.</span> The essay has moved around but at the time of
> writing is available at
> econ161.berkeley.edu/TCEH/Slouch_wealth2.html.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">The economist William Nordhaus: William
> Nordhaus, “Do Real-Output and Real-Wage Measures Capture Reality? The
> History of Lighting Suggests Not,” in Timothy F. Bresnahan and Robert
> J. Gordon, eds., <span class="italic">The Economics of New
> Goods</span> (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1997), available
> at econpapers.repec.org/paper/cwlcwldpp/1078.htm. Also see David
> Warsh, <span class="italic">Knowledge and the Wealth of
> Nations,</span> chapter 24, which describes Nordhaus’s
> experiments.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>
> <span class="calibre4">Subsistence income: See Tim Harford, “Cash 22,”
> <span class="italic">FT Magazine,</span> March 31, 2007, and Abhijit
> V. Banerjee and Esther Duflo, “The Economic Lives of the Poor,” MIT
> Working Paper, October 2006, econ-www.mit.edu/files/530.</span>
>
> <div class="calibre14">
>
>  
>
> </div>
>

<span class="calibre7 bold">ABOUT THE AUTHOR</span>

<div class="calibre6">

 

</div>

> > <div class="calibre11">
> >
> > <span class="calibre4">T<span class="calibre13">IM</span>
> > H<span class="calibre13">ARFORD</span> is a member of the
> > <span class="italic">Financial Times</span> editorial board. His
> > column, “The Undercover Economist,” which reveals the economic ideas
> > behind everyday experiences, is published in the
> > <span class="italic">Financial Times</span> and
> > <span class="italic">Slate.</span> He is also the only economist in
> > the world to run a problem page, “Dear Economist” in it,
> > <span class="italic">Financial Times</span> readers’ personal
> > problems are answered tongue-in-cheek with the latest economic
> > theory. Tim hosted the BBC television series
> > <span class="italic">Trust Me, I’m an Economist</span> and now hosts
> > the BBC radio series <span class="italic">More or Less.</span> He is
> > a regular contributor to <span class="italic">Marketplace</span> and
> > the BBC World Service, and his writing has appeared in
> > <span class="italic">Esquire, Forbes, New York</span> magazine,
> > <span class="italic">The Washington Post,</span> and
> > <span class="italic">The New York Times.</span> Tim won the 2006
> > Bastiat Prize for economic journalism. Before becoming a writer, Tim
> > worked for Shell, the World Bank, and as a tutor at Oxford
> > University. He lives in London with his wife and two daughters.
> > Visit his website at <a href="http://www.timharford.com"
> > class="calibre9">www.timharford.com</a>.</span>
> >
> > </div>
